i-HwDELM5jdrafK4qwpxh6P	Madam President, I would like Today, we are debating the report of the Committee on Finance 17 on amended rules to allow the disclosure of data for income studies. The Government has submitted a proposal to amend the Act on Official Statistics and the Act on Publicity and Confidentiality. The background is that SCB has assessed for the last 15 years that there is no legal support in Swedish legislation to disclose Swedish data to LIS. Therefore, the most recent transmission of data from Sweden submitted to LIS relates to 2005. LIS is an abbreviation for Luxembourg Income Study, which is a non-profit data and research centre based in Luxembourg and with a branch office at The City University of New York, USA. The research centre is considered a highly respected organisation in the research community and aims to enable, facilitate, promote and conduct research on socio-economic outcomes and the institutional conditions that contribute to the creation of these outcomes. LIS operates and provides two databases dedicated to research on income distribution, poverty and wealth distribution. In order to analyse the distribution of income at household or individual level, data is needed at the level of the individual. The LIS database therefore contains data on individuals' income, tax payments, social security, public contributions and certain costs from nearly 50 countries. For these tasks to be meaningful, they need to be linked to personal data such as gender, age, marital status, etc. Today's report contains a reservation from the Swedish Democrats and the Moderates to reject the bill and that the government should come back with a proposal that limits the disclosure of data and that there should be assurances that the data provided cannot be directly attributed to an individual. The Government proposal states that the data should not be attributable to any individual and that sensitive personal data should not be disclosed, except for information on social security benefits in the event of illness or occupational injury. However, all data will be disclosed in de-identified form. In other words, it will not be possible to assign a certain integrity sensitive task to an individual. And the data will be processed by LIS exclusively for statistical purposes and in accordance with the provisions of the EU Data Protection Regulation. Today, data are released in all Nordic countries except Sweden, but also in most other EU countries as well as Switzerland, the United Kingdom, the United States, Canada and Australia, to name but a few. Over the past 30 years, there has been a trend of rising income inequality in many developing countries. Several international organisations, such as the IMF, the OECD and the World Bank, have been more interested in issues relating to income distribution. Both the UN and the EU have therefore set targets on poverty and the spread of income. The microdata that LIS collects can provide a more complete picture and improve the ability to further analyse and evaluate the impact of economic policy decisions. We Social Democrats, like the government, believe that it is of great importance for society as a whole to be able to study and analyse this and, not least, to use it as a basis for political decisions. This applies to research and teaching purposes as well as to the general debate on, for example, economic policy, income distribution and income development in general and in different groups of the population. I therefore support the proposal in the report and reject the reservation.

i-CdDDZiWMLcY4oKF64vYpGe	Madam President, I would like The report we are dealing with does not bear the startling title of Amended rules to allow the disclosure of data for income studies. It may seem quite peripheral and technically and hardly worth saying anything about. It is a matter whose background – and also the motion that has been made on the basis of the bill, and which wants to reject it – says some important things about developments in Sweden over the past 15 years and the state of Swedish politics today. The decision itself is only about introducing a legislative regulation that allows the relevant authority, reasonably SCB, to once again supply statistics to international comparisons within the framework of Luxembourg Income Study after a 15-year break. It is a major non-commercial international project with two important databases for research on income distribution, wealth distribution and poverty development. The situation that we can now finally remedy arose at one time as a side effect of Sweden's abolition of the wealth tax early in the Reinfeldt government. It was after this that SCB considered that there was no legal support for the provision of information. I note, moreover, that this background is not included in the proposal itself or in the report. In any case, it is a very regrettable situation, not to say embarrassing. I remember a visit to Sweden in 2015 by the great British economist Tony Atkinson. He is the one who basically established a research field for studies of inequality in income and wealth and its effects. On that occasion he had come out with a book, the one that was to be his last. He was eager, and perhaps most of all, to make an appeal to the assembled ones to do something about the very strange situation. Sweden, which from the start of the income studies in 1983 had been involved in delivering high quality statistics, had now ceased to do so. This, of course, creates holes in time series and lowers the quality of the statistics for all researchers around the world who use the databases to better understand the development. But it also means at home that we get a little worse at understanding by, among other things, putting the development here in Sweden in an international context. Nevertheless, we know from other compilations of facts and statistics that developments in Sweden in this area have been very dramatic. Sweden is one of the countries in the industrialized world where the gap has increased fastest in the last 25 to 30 years. Sweden is a country that today stands out in a negative way when it comes to strong concentration of wealth in a narrow layer at the top, with the ensuing negative effects on social development that it gives and that has been studied for decades of international research. Sweden has also, in contrast to how it was the first 40 to 45 years after the Second World War and also in contrast to what is still a lingering picture in many in and outside Sweden, become a country where heavy systems are rigged for widening divides and for transferring resources upward in the economic pyramid. As I said, the fact that we are standing here today is a side effect of precisely the abolition of wealth tax. Then we should remember that when it comes to capital taxation in some respects, Sweden has become something of a tax haven in European comparison by having neither property tax, inheritance tax and gift tax nor property taxation based on the commercial value of properties. Sweden is quite extreme, and then unfortunately no longer in a good way but in a bad way. To return to the report, I note that there have been many bodies and organisations that have long called for the legal problem to be resolved so that the statistics can be delivered from SCB to the international databases. Nevertheless, the question has been drawn in the long term. I believe that there was a strong lack of interest from the bourgeois government which first caused the problem. The Left Party has exercised this in parliament before and also raised the issue with the government during negotiations last parliamentary term. It is good that this is finally happening. As we have heard here, the Swedish Democrats have exercised that the bill should be rejected. We heard from the speaker's chair that it is in principle a question of scepticism that statistics at all exist on various important conditions in Sweden. It is in line with that party's way of being in the world. In this case, it is a rather jumbled argument that is put forward. Reference is made to privacy and the data risk not being unidentified. In the bill, the proposed legal text is admittedly: a U pptgifter should only be released to the extent necessary to reflect Swedish conditions in the research databases provided by the organisation. The data shall not be directly attributable to any individual. So this is the text of the law. It is also interesting that personal integrity and the risk of misuse of information about individuals' practices in the vast majority of other situations in society usually mean very little to the very party that has submitted the motion, the Swedish Democrats, and, more recently, to the party that is involved as a drag on the rejection, the Moderates. When it comes to a question where the information might at some point lead to an analysis or a research effort whose results can be a little annoying to some of the richest in our society, it is an objection that is dusted off, even though in this case there is hardly anything that has actual bearing. The eavesdropping of people without concrete crime suspicion is quite fine. That is something you want to crank up in the general debate. But delivering Swedish statistics to an international database where someone can find out that the rich in Sweden have become richer is a threat to personal privacy. That is quite remarkable. This is something that goes well with a greater political trend in our time. It is parties like the Swedish Democrats who want to be the most serviceable for the economic and social interests of the most wealthy. This is something that we have seen in everything from the defence of profit loss from the welfare sector to tax policy more generally and more. With this note, I would like to support the committee's proposal in the report.

i-Qo8FpkyAssnNvQRfjyDPN4	Madam President, I would like In order to achieve efficiency in administration and to conserve state resources, it is important that the right authority does the right things. The purpose of this is to determine which authorities are not socially useful or whether their actual mission has been overshadowed by irrelevant considerations. The general starting point should be that, in addition to serving the public, authorities are there to enforce political decisions and to inform or investigate specific matters within the authority's sphere of activity. Politics, on the other hand, must be conducted in the parliamentary assemblies and in the general debate. Authorities that are politicised or redundant or whose tasks overlap with other, more efficient authorities should be dismantled or reformed in order to focus on their core tasks. i-34GwXDLx1shGPGhMdaNyap	Madam President, I would like There is much to be said about state administration, but what is contained in this report as a result of general exercise is more limited. I was going to confine myself to the subject raised by the Left Party in its reservation, reservation number 8, which I also take the opportunity to call for approval. It concerns the recruitment of temporary workers in state activities. This is something that has been drawn attention from several quarters, particularly trade unions, over the years. For example, the ST Trade Union, which organises government officials, has on various occasions highlighted the problems posed by the state's use of temporary agencies and similar solutions. It can be observed that the State is using temporary agency work more extensively than when the possibility was introduced at the time, and that there has been a change in the content of temporary agency work. Temporary agency workers and the like are now no longer used only in the support functions of public authorities such as reception, change and administration, but also for certain work in the core activities of public authorities which may actually be the exercise of public authority. In this context, it is important that the risk of the public's opportunities for transparency, control and accountability being impaired be taken seriously, as well as the risk of negative consequences for the quality of the management. I would argue that this is one of several structural threats to the Swedish administrative model and, in the long run, to its legitimacy. It should be quite clear that there are problems with private law agencies becoming part of the core business of public authorities. I would like to highlight two additional problems or risks. Firstly, the use of temporary agency work creates a division of staff where temporary workers have a higher threshold for participation and influence over the job. This affects both the work itself and the ability to run a functioning trade union activity, just as it is in other types of activities. Secondly, the widespread renting-in involves a large cost to the State. Now it has been a few years, but during 2016, for example, ST's Federal Journal published a review of some fifteen government employers and their use of temporary agencies. That review showed that in several authorities there were consultants who had been hired full-time for several years. According to the study, several authorities also depended on expensive consultants, which means large costs for the State. This review also showed that a large part of the agency staff carried out official activities. The Left Party believes that the government's use of temporary agencies should be phased out. It is partly about the state being a good employer, and partly about safeguarding the public authority's activities. A first step could be to clarify this in the employers' policy of the state authorities. It is desirable for the State to act as a positive example in matters of principle concerning employers. If the state takes the lead and ensures that it avoids problematic staffing solutions in its own personnel supply, it can hopefully also spread to other parts of the labour market. Madam President, I would like This is what the Left Party's motion and reservation in this report is about. I would like to emphasise that this more specific problem of staffing contracts is part of a wider issue of government administration and how the state and the public generally manage the skills and experience of their authorities. A few years ago, there were writings about a British minister in the Conservative Government, a word of Agnew. He had warned, in an internal letter leaking, that the extensive procurement of consulting services in the state apparatus led to the infantilisation of this apparatus. This is not a unique British thing, but there is reason to think about how such things can happen in Sweden, for example. For example, we can look at the ongoing so-called reform of labour market policy, which is actually a forced further transformation of the Public Employment Service into a procurement and control shell. In addition to the obvious and serious risks of commercial interests drumming the needs of the unemployed and the deterioration of service in sparsely populated parts of the country, we also see how crucial competence risks disappearing completely from the state as such. Job intermediaries who have developed concrete knowledge of local conditions and skills of a kind are already replaced by staff who will primarily guide people to web forms and on to other actors. The knowledge and skills that may be generated in the process of equipping and matching the unemployed will in future be much more fragmented as potential competitive advantages for more or less serious companies. The state's ability to pursue active labour market policies is atrophy. For example, in a completely different end of government activity, we can find maintenance of rail. The far-reaching fragmentation and outsourcing of maintenance have been causing significant problems for a long time. This is not surprising, because the railways are a coherent system, and cutting up maintenance with commercial profit and yield requirements on the different parts poses problems at the system level. But it also means that both specialized machines and definite knowledge and skills are spread out and away from the state, which, however, remains responsible in some kind of abstract sense but with limited concrete governance possibilities. Nevertheless, it is all too rarely, I would say, that there is any major critical discussion about what outsourcing and fragmentation of this kind is doing to our public administration. The idea that some kind of abstract market mechanisms are by definition effective and that one only needs to make sure that smart purchases and controls are made to casha in thrive at the command heights of public Sweden. This brings, I mean, a flat and antisocial view of knowledge and skills at all. It is believed that they are disconnected from actual people and actual organisations within the public sector. This is a discussion that there is every reason to return to many times also in other contexts in this House.

Why should they have to pay a fee for checks that may not even have been carried out, since the supervision itself was carried out only every two or three years? The regulation focuses on official controls in the food chain, i.e. supervision of food controls. Simplifying the regulatory framework makes it easy for both new and old operators to know what applies, regardless of the sector in which they operate. i-TCqm4oEaE6y1tahvRQJbUq	Madam President, I would like Today is a historic day for consumer policy, because today we are adopting a new law on consumer purchases – the latest one came to 1990. By implementing this new law, we also take care of two EU directives. Certainly, consumer law needs to be modernised, because the way we act as consumers has changed drastically. Digitization is progressing at a furious rate and can simplify our lives, but sometimes it causes us to tear our hair. We are also global consumers in a completely different way, and this cross-border way of consuming must be taken into account by law. Today we are taking major steps towards more comprehensive and better consumer protection legislation, and the law is appropriately proposed to enter into force on 1 May this year. The most important change in the new Consumer Sales Law is that the reversal of the burden of proof is extended from six months to two years. It may sound technical but it is about something as simple as important, namely consumer power. This change means that any errors that appear during two years after buying a product as a starting point shall be considered to be faults for which the trader is responsible. So it is the company that should prove that the headphones you bought had nothing wrong from the beginning. Legislation is now creating an important incentive to produce sustainable products and not build up planned ageing, which rogue companies can do to make money. With the law on their side, consumers should be more easily able to complain about bad headphones, the dress whose seams went up at the third use or the bag whose zipper broke. This is important because, as the vast majority of us know, we need to reduce our climate footprint. Let me take clothing as an example. A double life span of textile products could halve the climate impact of Swedes, and it is important if we want a planet to leave to our children and grandchildren. I know that this will make a difference for consumers, and I know this because both authorities and consumer organisations are in favour of this proposal. This is a crucial change in which we take a stand against the slit-and-slip society and for consumers and the climate. It is easy to see who is on the side of consumers here today. C and KD do not think that the consumer power needs to be strengthened, and they believe that we are overimplementing. However, we say that both consumers and the climate deserve better, and that is why today we are going to adopt a new consumer purchasing law. Madam President, I would like Digitalization has changed the way we live, and we stream and look for Pokémon as never before. Here too, legislation needs to be updated and a new EU directive implemented. An important part of this is the obligation to update the trader. The trader shall inform and provide the consumer with safety updates and other updates necessary for the functioning of the service. This is valid for three years or as long as the agreement is in force. An error arising from a digital content shall be the trader's responsibility as a starting point. On the other hand, the consumer must ensure that equipment is capable of performing the service and cooperate with the trader if there is a need for troubleshooting. Madam President, I would like Animals are our family members, and it is important that we have legal regulation for the purchase of living beings. The Government proposes that the purchase of live animals by consumers should continue to be subject to consumer purchasing legislation, but specific rules should be introduced. We do this because we know that you buy a dog or a horse from a breeder, not in a pet store. There are parties who say in an announcement that the Sales Law would be better suited as legislation. However, we believe that the Sales Law would give the buyer of the animal a far too vulnerable position, because in the Sales Law you can cancel your rights. Consumer protection is therefore weaker. The purchase of a beloved pet and family member must be more protected than that, and so we say that the mandatory rules of the Consumer Sales Law provide stronger and better protection. The right to compensation in the event of an animal's failure may then be greater. This can lead to suffering for people and the new family member if an injury is not compensated, so that the animal does not receive the necessary veterinary care, and at worst the animal may need to be killed. Anyone who has lost an animal knows what a tremendous loss it can be to the family. We therefore want strong consumer contracts that are regulated by a binding consumer purchasing law. It's the best thing for both humans and animals. However, we understand that living animals are not just any commodity. The condition of an animal can change. In the case of more expensive animals, we therefore propose a study rule. As with other large purchases in life, a consumer should carefully examine the animal, and unless the trader has gone against faith and honour in a reckless manner, error should not be invoked. Since animals are living beings, the reverse burden of proof is suggested to be shorter than when buying a commodity. An error occurring within six months shall be deemed to have occurred at the time of delivery. The Committee on the Environment and Agriculture considered the government's model to be wise. i-JQ9gP9Gxj2Au338ev1VwWB	Madam President, I would like We Moderates appreciate the government's initiative for a new Consumer Purchase Act and support much of the content. On three points, however, follow-up and some change are required in order for the law to achieve its purpose without affecting Sweden's consumers or Sweden's competitiveness negatively. With regard to the burden of proof on the trader, the Government proposes that an error which has occurred within two years should be a fault for which the trader is responsible, unless the trader shows otherwise or this is incompatible with the nature of the goods or the nature of the defect. This means that the government proposes to extend the burden of proof for the trader, from today's six months to two years. The EU directive requires the burden of proof to cover the trader over a year, and legislating for another year is therefore an over-implementment, which we in Sweden have become the closest EU champion on. It is also an extension compared to what the investigation wanted to introduce when the bill was presented, when it proposed one year's burden of proof for the trader in accordance with the minimum level of the EU Directive. While we Moderates are in favour of strengthening consumer protection, binding legislation must always strike a balance between the two interests at stake. Extending the burden of proof to two years may be justified in order to strengthen consumer law, but we believe that the consequences of the legislation need to be evaluated after three years in order to examine the potential negative impact on Sweden's competitiveness that over-implementing can have. Furthermore, the Government proposes in the new Consumer Act that the consumer should have the right to require the trader to correct a defect in the goods or re-delivery and that the consumer should have the right to choose between the measures. We believe that there is reason to strengthen the consumer's right in this respect, but we believe that the government's proposal is too far-reaching. We believe that the trader should be able to take the measure that can be implemented at the lowest cost as long as it is not an inconvenience to the consumer. Swedish horse breeding is well regarded in the world. Swedish-born horses are exported abroad where they reap good success, and breeding of horses is also one of the mainstays of the Swedish horse industry. Raising horses also means a great deal to society at large in terms of jobs, rural businesses, open landscapes and more. At the moment, however, breeders are in a very difficult situation with regard to one of their most central activities, namely the sale of horses. Swedish horse industry, especially the large horseback riding sector, consists mainly of people who use the horse for hobby or other recreational purposes. This means that the main market for Swedish horse breeders is among private individuals, while the majority of breeders are engaged in economic activities. For the sale of a horse between a trader and a private person, it is today the Consumer Sales Law that governs the purchase in the event of a complaint. The law is designed to protect individuals who, in trade with traders, can be very vulnerable. However, the current design may have disproportionate consequences for traders in trade in live animals. The problem is that many horse breeders are currently highly hesitant about selling horses to private individuals. Without a change, this means that Swedish farmers face a very real risk of losing their largest market: sales to private individuals. If the current trend continues, Swedish horse breeding will stop, and there is a risk of having a number of negative long-term effects such as reduced rural entrepreneurship, loss of revenue for the industry and job losses. Therefore, we now welcome the fact that there is a majority in Parliament for an announcement on a change in the Consumer Sales Law as regards the purchase of more expensive animals. By way of conclusion, I support reservation 5.
i-E85KrGJtBQc3XMwaL5rKgQ	Madam President, I would like I think we can all agree in this House that a cat is not a dishwasher and that a horse cannot be equated with a toaster. Yet that is exactly what our legislation does. In the Swedish legal system, animals are legally regarded as movable property, i.e. things. This does not correspond with how many people today view their animals, and it is also quite obvious that animals are affected by inheritance and environment in a completely different way from, for example, a mobile phone. It is therefore not reasonable to continue to equate animals with dead things, but the focus must be on animal welfare. Unfortunately, this is where the government's proposal for a new consumer purchasing law misses the target. Today's legislation on the purchase of live animals does not meet the animal welfare requirements that Sweden should meet. As the law is designed at present, its aim is to protect consumers' interests, not those of animals. Of course, this is good when it comes to other goods, but there is a strange order when it comes to the purchase of live animals. Since the consumer has the same rights in the case of defects in the goods, the reversal of the burden of proof and the time limit for complaint as in the case of a purchase of any other goods, there are some problems. What happens, for example, if after two years a buyer wants to complain about his injured horse and get a new one? The risk of prolonged processes where animals are at risk of being squeezed increases. The care obligation that applies when the buyer either wishes to cancel the purchase, to demand that an error be remedied or to receive a re-delivery often means in practice that the animal does not receive the care it needs, despite compliance with the Animal Welfare Act, because the buyer usually does not want to spend more time or money on the animal in question. A horse that is turned off and is not trained or competed with risks having its original injury aggravated, other injuries or impaired mental health. In addition, a seller may be forced to buy back an animal in damaged condition after many years and may be liable to pay damages of large amounts, sometimes exceeding the value. This uncertainty about the burden of proof and the time limit for complaints concerning live animals makes many breeders, especially small traders, reluctant to raise animals to sell to private individuals. There is a considerable financial risk of long liability and the farmer has limited opportunity to prove that an error is due to circumstances on the part of the buyer. These conditions contribute to the continuation of illegal imports of animals, as the demand for dogs in particular on the Swedish market is greater than supply. The importation of these animals often takes place from so-called puppy factories where the animals are treated very badly. It can also lead to financial difficulties for individuals, as uncertainties arise as to who should bear veterinary costs if the imported animal needs care. The investigation that forms the basis for the proposal for a new Consumer Purchase Act that the Government is now putting on the board of the Riksdag clearly describes the current problems. However, the government lacks an adequate solution to these problems. The purpose of the Consumer Sales Law is to level out the strength ratio between buyer and seller. The law thus focuses on the fact that a consumer is usually at a disadvantage vis-à-vis a trader. This is the case in most cases, but not when it comes to the purchase of live animals from breeders. Unlike large retail chains and other businesses, many breeders have livestock farming as a hobby, not as their main source of income. You are counted as a trader, but you do not have the advantage that normally applies. One who has planned to buy an animal for thousands of dollars has probably read about the breed and learned what it takes to care for that particular animal. He or she thus finds himself or herself in a position of knowledge equivalent to that of the seller and is usually also economically equal, if not superior. All in all, therefore, it is a good thing that this legislation is now being reviewed, as is happening in connection with the introduction of these two EU directives. However, it is not enough, as the government proposes, to keep the purchase of live animals under the Consumer Sales Law and only allow certain exceptions. Such a regime will continue to create problems with demarcation and also makes the already tight legislation on consumer purchases even more difficult to navigate. Instead, clear contractual terms between buyers and sellers are needed to avoid unnecessary suffering for animals. We therefore say no to the government's proposal to continue treating horses and dogs in the same way as toasters and kitchen chairs. Madam President, I would like Sweden's animal welfare must be characterised by high ambitions. The government therefore needs to take Parliament's announcement seriously and fall to little for the criticism that has been levelled at the proposal. If the Social Democrats share the view that animal welfare and safety are not a secondary issue, they must urgently come back with proposals for a change of legislation that fully meets the animal welfare perspective and reduces the risk of unnecessary processing. Mahatma Gandhi said: A nation's greatness and its ethical progress can be judged by how to treat its animals.

The longer a product is used and the more it is used, the better it is for the environment. This is also why the Center Party wants to be able to offer a consumer first and foremost who discovers a fault that the fault of the product is remedied– repaired– before it is replaced, in relation to sustainability issues. Similar suggestions can be found in the Moderate reservation, where the starting point should be that the seller determines whether the goods should be repaired or replaced. We believe, like the Moderates, that this is not something that should be regulated by law, which one chooses to do. Finally, Madam President, the functioning of digital content depends entirely on the environment in which it has been installed. If the hardware or systems with which it interacts are changed, it must be possible to upgrade over a long period of time. This applies in particular to safety-critical systems and those containing sensitive data. Today it is possible to buy a smartphone that will not be able to be security updated after it has left the store. This means, in practice, that it is sold with open security holes from day one. It makes it difficult for an individual to protect himself and his information. The same applies to other types of hardware, including those purchased by public operators. Society imposes requirements on consumer safety in a wide range of areas, but not in the area of safety. It is time to change that and in consumer law and at least in the case of public procurement to require updating guarantees of three years or the entire life span of the product if this is shorter. Madam President, I would like The consumer area is developing rapidly, and we must respect the fact that it can be difficult for legislation to keep up with – mainly in the area of digital services and digital content, where technological developments are fast moving in different directions. Safety and protection against intrusion is important, as is protecting children from downloading content that harms their self-perception and gives them to those who want to exploit their situation and their young age. Madam President, I would like I am in favour of the Center Party's reservation No 8 on digital content, but of course I support our three other reservations – and of course also the majority of the committee, which opposes the regulation of the purchase of live animals being made in the Consumer Sales Law.
i-MU6CPrk6afCHLtZDHzBQXP	Madam President, I would like Live animals should not be considered as any goods, because they are not. They're alive, with flesh and blood. Then it does not work that the Consumer Sales Law equates a purchase of a live animal with the purchase of goods such as computers, refrigerators and clothes. The Consumer Sales Law forgets the animal, I would argue. It does not take sufficient account of the fact that a living animal is changing and strongly influenced by its surroundings. Therefore, these purchases should go under the Sales Law. The government proposes to introduce certain specific rules for the purchase of live animals by consumers. The specific rules proposed shall apply, in particular, to the purchase of more expensive animals, and for these purchases a duty of investigation shall be imposed on the consumer. The government also proposes to maintain a six-month presumption rule for the purchase of animals, while in the case of the purchase of more expensive animals, the presumption rule should be completely waived. It is a step in the right direction, we believe Christian Democrats, but it is not enough. The animal industry has long criticised the way the Consumer Sales Law treats live animals like any product. Animals are not just any commodity but living beings where psyche and physics interact and where different circumstances affect how they behave. Against this background, it is understandable that the government is proposing to introduce specific rules for the purchase of live animals. The Christian Democrats welcome the Government's proposal that the presumption rule should be agreed off when buying more expensive animals and that a duty of investigation should be imposed on the consumer. However, we are not satisfied but believe that it is not enough. In the animal industry, there has long been great dissatisfaction that the purchase of live animals is regulated in the Consumer Sales Law. When selling expensive animals, the balance of power between the parties is often not as clear as it is in a traditional relationship between the trader and the consumer. Moreover, the sale of expensive animals often takes place on a small scale where it is not always given in what capacity the parties act, as traders and consumers or as private individuals. The Christian Democrats' view is that live animals should not be included in the scope of the Consumer Sales Law but that the Sales Law should instead be applied to these transactions. This is a change that has long been requested by the animal industry through, among other things, the Swedish Veterinary Association, the National Federation of Farmers and the Swedish Kennel Club. The unpredictability resulting from the Consumer Sales Law has made animal industry increasingly reluctant to sell expensive animals to consumers, which is not good. The Christian Democrats believe that this is problematic because it can have significant negative effects on the animal industry. It is also problematic because it risks causing consumers to enter into trade in animals brought in from abroad in violation of an import ban or under other uncertain conditions. The Swedish Purchasing Act places higher demands on the buyer to find out more about the animal before the purchase. It is something that is desirable when it comes to living animals because it is not a commodity among everyone else. If the buyer finds out more about the animal before the transaction takes place, there will be fewer disputes, which will benefit both the buyer and the seller. Not least, it is for the benefit of the animal, who is often in danger of getting into trouble in long and drawn-out disputes. The most exciting thing that has happened today in this debate – it has been going on for a while – is that the Social Democrats have admitted that they have not been on the side of the consumer since 1990. They say that on the side of the consumer is only the one who believes that the burden of proof has been reversed for two years. Since 1990 we have had a law which says that the reversal of the burden of proof is six months, the Social Democrats have acknowledged that they have not been on the side of the consumer during this time. We Christian Democrats and the Center Party, which have proposed extending it from six months to one year, are all of a sudden the enemies of the consumer. I find it indecent to use such words in this chair. We can both say that we are looking after the consumer, but I think we also need to see that there is a harmony with traders and consumers and what the different parties can do. One year we think and the Center Party is fine. We think it could be better for the consumer, but we also believe that the trader could do that. I therefore support reservation number 3.
i-YKVUowmK63qstatnFzLM4A	Madam President, I would like We in the Green Party know that several of the changes that we have worked out together with the government in this bill reinforce the power of the consumer in several ways. It's good and important. However, we did not get through our desire to remove animals from the Consumer Sales Law and instead work out a special law dealing with the purchase and sale of live animals. It would have been absolutely best both for the animals and for the buyer. It is a pity that the government did not take advantage of the possibility of excluding the sale of live animals in accordance with the possibility provided for in EU Directive 2019/ 771. There are many reasons why live animals should be removed from the Consumer Sales Law. We've already heard a lot of people here today. Having taken note of all the referrals received on the proposal for a new Consumer Purchase Act, I see that the proposal now on the table has taken very little account of what various animal welfare organisations have advocated as changes. What we came up with when we deepened the issue is that there is a need for specific legislation in the area of trade in live animals. Therefore, a thorough investigation needs to be carried out with the aim of producing a legislative proposal that deals with the purchase and sale of live animals, where the welfare of the animals is in focus but of course also what the buyer's protection can look like. It has never been done before, and the effects on animal welfare on the purchase and sale of animals are tangible. The purchase regulation is designed for goods whose quality and characteristics can be measured and guaranteed in different ways. As I said, animals are living beings, and the regulation is therefore ill-suited to trade in live animals. A similar conclusion was reached when the Consumer Services Act was introduced. Animals are in themselves property and could therefore fall within the scope of the Consumer Services Act, but those rules are adapted for so-called performance services. Since work on live animals is characterized by a duty to show care rather than to perform a certain result, live animals were excluded from the scope of the law. For example, it is possible to fix a broken water pump on a car so that the car works again, but it is not the same way to fix an animal by, for example, removing an object that is stuck in the intestines with the guarantee that the animal will feel good again. A veterinarian promises to do his best and use established methods, while the mechanic promises to make the car work again. It is usually said that legislation should be predictable and that it will be precisely that by being uniform. The fact that the Consumer Services Act does not cover live animals is therefore an argument that neither the purchasing rules should. I have more short examples: it is not possible to declare animals in order for them to apply over time, as one thing can do. The replacement of one live animal for another is not possible either, as there are no equivalent copies. Nor can the characteristics of a living animal be guaranteed or presumed in the same way as for other goods. The limit for what counts as commercial animals should also be reviewed. Customs has requested that the limit for commercial animals be lowered from five dogs to two dogs, to make it more difficult for smugglers and so on. There are different ways to solve the problem. An investigator should be instructed immediately to investigate what a bill dealing with the purchase and sale of live animals might look like. I therefore call for the approval of Amendment No 11 in this report by the Committee on Civil Liberties and Internal Affairs.

i-74fBD4LHgdQfFziFEXi5XA	Madam President, I would like Thank you for the opportunity to come and take part in the debate! This is the first debate in which I take part. I'm honored to be here. Madam President, I would like Now I go to today's theme: a new consumer purchasing law. It's a historic day. It has been said on several occasions in the speaker's chair. The introduction of a new Consumer Purchase Act is one of the largest developments in consumer law in 15 to 20 years. It will strengthen the protection of all of us consumers. The new consumer legislation means that Swedish consumers are given stronger protection when purchasing both physical and digital products. By making it easier for consumers to advertise goods, the new Consumer Sales Law helps to make products more sustainable for both the planet and the wallet. I will focus on three important changes today: firstly, how we strengthen consumer law in the digital forum; secondly, how we encourage sustainable products by extending the statutory guarantee; and thirdly, the part that makes it clear that the purchase of animals is not a purchase of any commodity. Madam President, I would like The current Consumer Sales Law was decided by Parliament in 1990. Since then, the pattern of consumption has changed drastically. The Internet has entered, we have smart phones and our habits have changed in a way that few could imagine in the early 90s. There was no Internet commerce, no streaming services and no social media in anyone's everyday life. Today, we see much of this as necessities. It is high time we took the Consumer Sales Law into the digital era and strengthened consumer protection. Perhaps the most important change now proposed is new rules for the purchase of digital content and services. More and more of what we consume are different forms of digital products. It concerns the purchase of individual music or film files, the use of streaming services and access to social networks. The introduction of this new law also introduces clear rules on the liability of digital content providers vis-à-vis consumers and on the rights we have in the event of errors in digital content. In addition, there are clear rules on the obligations of traders to keep digital content up-to-date and well-functioning after it has been sold to a consumer. More and more of the traditional goods we are buying today are largely dependent on a well-functioning software. The new Act also contains rules that traders are obliged to keep such software up to date in the same way as applies to digital content sold separately. These are rules that give consumers greater security. The life of purchased goods shall not be limited by keeping the digital parts that control them up to date. An important part of this legislation is that we now make it clear that the use of social media is also a form of consumption where, instead of paying with money, we pay with our personal data. Today, it is far too unclear what applies to the use of social media. The new rules make it clear that consumer protection rules apply when you agree with a company to use a service in exchange for their access to your personal data and data for commercial purposes. Madam President, I would like Another important difference since the 1990s is that we have understood to a greater extent the extent of the challenges we face when it comes to climate change that humanity creates. One of the three priorities of the government is that Sweden should lead the climate transition, both to create a long-term sustainable society with jobs in Sweden and to be an example in the world. To achieve this, we need to change consumption behaviour. We must get away from toil and toss and move towards more sustainable consumption. Too many of us have experienced that products do not meet the standards. It's both frustrating and environmentally damaging. This is an important reason for strengthening consumer law. With greater rights for consumers, it becomes less profitable to sell products that do not keep their promises. In the case of traditional physical goods, we want to extend the period when you have the right to have a product repaired or to have a new product if the company cannot prove that it is the customer who caused the defect. It acts as a statutory guarantee, and instead of being valid for six months today, it shall be valid for two years. It is not reasonable for expensive products – we are talking about mobile phones and computers – to crash within two years. We now want to make it easier for the consumer to complain about such goods. If a product breaks down within two years of normal use, the seller will have an obligation to replace or repair the product. This strengthens consumer protection and also discourages unsustainable consumption, because when companies have to take greater responsibility for keeping products, it becomes less profitable to produce products of inferior quality. This means that the new consumer legislation, which is now on Parliament's table, can contribute to a better climate by transforming companies into more sustainable production. Madam President, I would like Buying an animal is not like buying any good. An animal is a living creature. It is therefore reasonable to introduce in some parts a separate regulation for such purchases. We must also remember, then, that the purchase of an animal, such as a horse or a dog, is a huge financial investment for consumers but, of course, also emotionally. It would not be reasonable to completely exclude the purchase of animals from consumer protection rules. The rules now proposed by the government are both a duty of inquiry for the consumer and a time for the reversal of the burden of proof of six months. It will also be possible to cancel the reverse burden of proof when buying more expensive animals. This strikes me as a reasonable balance between the interests of the parties and a proposal that takes into account the interests of animal welfare. It is definitely a reinforcement in relation to what exists today in Sweden. Madam President, I would like With this new consumer legislation, Swedish consumers gain stronger protection when purchasing both physical and digital products. And with today's decision we take the Consumer Sales Law into the digital era. By making it easier for consumers to advertise goods, we contribute to the new Consumer Sales Law helping to produce products that are more sustainable for both the planet and the wallet. Finally, specific rules are proposed for the purchase of animals which take account of the fact that an animal is not just any commodity. All of this is important in order to strengthen consumer protection. It is also important to strengthen Sweden.

i-PmnxoZBEwLgK6bhqkdQpfR	Madam President, I would like The Moderates believe in the enlightened consumer, that free individuals should be allowed to make their own decisions about their consumption in a free market and that there should be secure legislation that protects the individual from deception and fraud. An important starting point for Moderate consumer policy is that consumers should be able to assimilate information that is reliable about the price and characteristics of products, as well as on production methods, safety aspects and how consumption affects people, the environment and the climate. By being up-to-date, a consumer has the opportunity to solve issues that arise in connection with consumption. An informed consumer should be aware of both his or her rights and obligations. The Moderates believe that it does not benefit either consumers or society at large if consumers lack knowledge of what is expected of them in a purchase. The Moderates therefore want to see that the Swedish Consumer Agency is given overall responsibility, from preventive work to supervisory responsibility for debt advice. By allocating responsibility to an individual authority, the chain of responsibility should be clearer and the opportunities for the responsible authority to work proactively and reactively should be improved. We want the Swedish Consumer Agency to develop a plan for how to increase the level of knowledge, how to make best contact with schools and young people and what other types of preventive measures are relevant. Budget and debt counselling is statutory, and all municipalities are obliged to provide advice and instructions on budget and debt issues to debtors. It has been shown that budget and debt counselling differs significantly across the country and that some municipalities even lack this function. Queues for advice also differ significantly between municipalities and the extent of support. We need to change that. The Moderates want budget and debt counselling to be more equal across the country. It is important that everyone in Sweden can get the help they need to get out of their debt traps. Perhaps the single most important factor for an individual to be able to plan his or her finances is knowledge. Therefore, well-functioning consumer education work is important both for society and the national economy as well as for individuals. Changes in society and the economy at large mean that new demands are placed on consumers' knowledge, both about their rights and about the private economy as a whole. In order to meet the increase in over-indebtedness, it is essential to point out to consumers that there are not only rights but also obligations. There is a risk that consumers will get a distorted picture of the legal situation only if they are informed of their rights. Over the past few years, the Swedish Consumer Agency and the Swedish Enforcement Authority have successfully implemented targeted measures to early strengthen young people's knowledge of the economy, for example through educations Ekonomismart and Koll at the cash register. The Moderates consider it urgent that work on consumer guidance should continue and develop. Most municipalities in Sweden have municipal consumer advisors. In recent years there has been a major structural change in their work in many places that has been positive. We see that municipal consumer guides play a very important role in preventive work and in consumer knowledge as a whole. Not least, we see a great need among new arrivals, where debt problems have become relatively common. Therefore, the Moderates want to highlight the importance of local consumer guidance continuing to develop. There is a value in having consumer assistance at national level, but only local actors have detailed knowledge of the local problems. There are, of course, many more areas in which we see it as urgent that there are changes, but today I choose to stop at that and conclude by calling for the approval of reservation 3.

i-YSuZPydP1GKhLxdbUM7FB7	Madam President, I would like Thank you for the opportunity to come and participate in this debate too! We in Sweden have a long tradition of protecting consumers' rights and promoting environmentally, socially and economically sustainable consumption. Through a high level of consumer protection, we ensure the ability of ordinary people to stand up to irresponsible entrepreneurs while at the same time contributing to more sustainable development. In many respects, consumer policy is about ensuring that what may seem to be self-evident also comes into play on the market. Products are supposed to keep their promises. You should get what you pay for, and marketing should be truthful and show real on the product's content. Unserious operators should not be favoured at the expense of companies that want to do their own thing. Thus, well-functioning consumer legislation is not only about strengthening individual rights in the market, but also about contributing to healthy competition and a healthy and well-functioning market economy. The starting point for consumer law is that individual consumers have a more vulnerable situation than the traders they meet and therefore have the right to proportionately stronger protection than when traders do business among themselves. Today, I will outline the government's priorities for consumer policy and work in this area and also relate to several of the motions that have been dealt with in the committee report. I will start by addressing the challenges posed by digitised markets, not least for consumers with a lack of internet experience. The importance of consumer guidance throughout the country is a matter which the government takes very seriously and which, not least, my own party has highlighted in several contexts. I would also like to mention the serious problem of over-indebtedness, where the government has taken several initiatives. Finally, I will talk about the climate. Accelerating the green transition is one of the government's priorities, and changing consumption patterns are crucial if we are to achieve the emission reductions needed to live up to the Paris Agreement and to have a planet that we can stand for in the future. The government's consumer policy is part of this work. Madam President, I would like As we all know, the consumer market is constantly evolving, including through increasing e-commerce, new payment solutions and intensive marketing, especially in digital channels. Changes bring benefits to many consumers. However, for consumers with a lack of internet experience and perhaps limited knowledge of the Swedish language or small financial resources, rapid development may lead to difficulties in taking advantage of rights and making informed choices. This, in turn, can result in consumers suffering financial damage or even incur greater debts than they can pay back. A high level of consumer protection and good opportunities for authorities to intervene in the event of infringements is therefore very important. The government has today proposed new rules that include the possibility of imposing fines for more types of infringements of the consumer protection regulatory framework, including in terms of marketing. Madam President, I would like Countering over-indebtedness is another very important issue for the government and something that involves many in this House. The government has taken a number of initiatives to counter over-indebtedness. These include credit assessment requirements for smaller loans as well as authorisation requirements for consumer credit companies offering this type of loan. In 2018, consumer protection rules on high-cost credit were introduced and the requirement for the marketing of credit to be moderate. Furthermore, a ban on credit as a first option in e-commerce has been introduced. In November last year, the Government appointed an investigation to further counteract risky lending and over-indebtedness. It's a broad mission. To examine that such claims are not misleading is important work for the Swedish Consumer Agency. As regards the environmental impact of consumption, new initiatives are also under way. Today, the Commission is expected to take a decision on a proposal for more consumer power in the green changeover. Later this year, more consumer-related EU proposals, including environmental claims and real right to repair, are expected. The Swedish Consumer Agency is tasked with promoting the dissemination and further development of information about environmentally sustainable consumption through collaboration with other actors who want to contribute to the dissemination of such information. It is from this perspective that we want to see a new self-home movement emerge, where people with their own labour input, a small capital and a desire to own their housing can knock down their boposts. It is also positive that municipalities are now involved in identifying houses that have been abandoned, trying to get in touch with their owners and bring about a sale. Seven out of ten want to own their home, and in the long term it is also for those who have a lower income economically beneficial to do so. Ownership should not be a matter of class, but it has become so because of the current credit constraints and the lack of alternatives for housing savings. Rental rights should exist, Madam President, and be available to those who need them, but the lack of housing on the rent side is probably our biggest failure today. Black trade and exploitation of a weaker party always flourish in markets that do not work. There's gonna be an insider-outside problem, and we don't want to open lease deals to the criminal gangs. More and more cost-effective construction, but above all mobility and better use of existing housing, is the solution to the housing shortage. But there is no quick fix for better functioning housing markets as long as we so strongly defend the existing systems. That is why the reforms in the rental market that were about to be implemented with the January agreement were so important. More efficient and fairer leasing negotiations, free leasing in new production and a clearer reflection of the location, attractiveness and demand of the existing stock are changes that are needed – the sooner, the better. Instead of beaks and claws defending those with high incomes who are on attractive contracts with low use value rents in the inner city of Stockholm, the Center Party wants to look at those groups that currently find it most difficult to find good housing that suits their conditions. It is seniors, young people and students, and it is the economically vulnerable, poor households that cannot demand housing because of social problems, abuse or illness. The problems of overcrowding are difficult to repair afterwards. Children need to grow up in safe environments where they are seen, supported and can do their homework, stay at home and bring home friends. Many highlight Gårdsten in Gothenburg as a shining example. I'll do it again, but there are more examples. We must give people the chance to succeed and, at the same time, make housing careers in a homely environment that feels safe. Unfortunately, housing policy is not just struggling with system errors. Perhaps it is the lack of incentives for the construction industry to keep costs down and build quickly, cost-effectively and environmentally smartly that is holding innovation back. I might add that building subsidies as they have been designed are not a cost-pressing system but a system that helps to keep costs high. It is developed building methods, smart climate-friendly material choices and a transition to more industrial thinking in the construction industry that gives us reduced building costs. It is the Center Party's view that taxpayers' money should not be used to subsidise housing that can be built with market principles and normal yield requirements of the developers. I have also met those who now regret the abolition of investment aid for rental apartments. I can understand that this kind of sudden change is difficult to manage and that it can temporarily mean a stoppage in some places. However, I would like to say to them that I regret that, so far, these subsidies have been too general and mainly directed towards housing which would have been built anyway. For the Center Party to continue to support such a system of extensive subsidies, they must be directed specifically at the weakest markets and at those households in greatest need of housing. We therefore welcome the study on social housing policy presented today by Karolina Skog. However, we want to go further and continue to investigate how a new social housing sector could be created for Swedish conditions with other countries as positive role models. I can note that the concept is not equivalent here in Sweden, which is a pity. As a result of the simplified procedure, there are only a few reservations in the report. Of course, I support all our requests, including those which are simplified, but I only ask for the approval of reservation 2 on the revision of accounting rules, for which I argued at the outset.

We can introduce start-up loans that allow more people to buy their own home. We can introduce a planning bonus to the municipalities so that we get greater planning readiness around the country. We can introduce greater deductibility for renting part of a dwelling so that we use the square metres that are better available. We can review the requirements in virtually all areas so that a number of investigations do not have to be carried out. We can throw away the beach protection proposal that has now come in the trash. It is a product that will not solve any problems of housing construction. The Social Democrats can also take note of what is included in the report – a review of the accounting rules. In a market where the market value may be 50 or 60 % of the cost of production, the value of the property is currently required to be written down directly, which makes it impossible for many companies to build in these areas despite the need. If the company is allowed to write down the property for a longer period – for example seven years, as we have proposed – it will be easier to build even in weak markets where there is a need for new housing. I agree with Erik Nises: the Social Democrats need a new housing policy that meets today's problems. With those words, I support reservation number 1.

It is a housing reform that gives the weakest in the housing market a better chance of obtaining a dwelling. Dwellings shall consist of newly built needs dwellings or existing rental apartments converted into a special dwelling through a social distribution ratio in the housing agency. Once a dwelling has been converted into a needs housing unit, it must remain in the social housing stock so that we can continuously build up a new, strong social housing sector, which is currently lacking. Sweden today has all the characteristics of the bad social housing that has existed in many countries around the world. Stigmatized areas and properties meant only for the less well-off are one of the biggest failures in other countries' models for what they call social housing. But today we see the same problem in our millions of programme areas. We also have this here in our country. Instead, we propose a model where needs housing should be mixed with rental and tenant-ownership apartments in ordinary properties. Dwellings must be spread throughout municipalities but not built at all in any vulnerable or particularly vulnerable areas. It is therefore good quality apartments that are inculcated in our society – no slums and no slums. Housing should be of the same quality and meet the same requirements as any dwelling. The rent for needs housing shall be the same at the entrance to the contract as was previously the case for the apartment if it has been converted from a rental right to a needs apartment. In new production of needs housing, the rent is set at the same level as for similar needs housing in the area. The Social Democratic Government has carried out a series of reinforcements of the protection of tenants in the housing market. Among other things, we have taken measures against the black trade in rental contracts, unauthorised subletting and the withdrawal of over-rents from second-hand tenants. We also introduced renovation support that all property owners of multi-family buildings could apply for. The aim was to reduce the costs for tenant-owners and tenants during renovation. But just like the investment aid, the renovation aid disappeared when the right-wing conservatives pushed their budget through and agreed to clear the housing policy area from money. Interestingly, some of the parties that are now fighting over the housing shortage removed one of the most powerful tools we had to sustain the construction, that is, investment support. It would therefore be reasonable for some self-criticism from these parties, for it is not good enough to say that it is the fault of the sossians every time the housing building sinks. In this case it is definitely not. We have also introduced the possibility to intervene at an early stage against poor management of rental properties. It is an important tool that I really hope will be used in the municipalities to stop social dumping. In addition, we have put in place reinforced property protection for tenants in the event of a ruined apartment. It is welcome and long-awaited by the tenant movement, and it will strengthen the right of tenants when they are at their most vulnerable, for example when the apartment has burned. Madam President, I would like Let me be clear about market rents. Market rents lead to an increased redistribution from tenants to property owners, resulting in lower daily economics, causing people to consume less. We Social Democrats oppose market rents and believe that the Swedish rental negotiation model should be strengthened and developed. The problem in Sweden is the housing shortage, not the rent negotiation system. Madam President, I would like I will end by reminding you of the forthcoming elections. Housing is part of the common welfare policy, which is why we must jointly take responsibility for it. Madam President, I would like Moreover, I believe that profits in welfare should be banned.

i-SYVDVCrqxyLS5fn7LNGmh5	Madam President, I would like We Moderates want to get Sweden in order. This requires a policy aimed at contributing to a secure society in which the individual has the opportunity to shape his own life and make a class trip, so that it becomes more important where he is going than where he comes from. For this, a functioning housing market is a prerequisite. A functioning housing market enables young people to start adult life, for parents to buy a small house when the family has grown and for those who want to leave home to study or try a new job. Madam President, I would like Today, this is not a matter of course. The Swedish housing market is fundamentally dysfunctional. Instead of offering opportunities, it hampers both individuals and the economy as a whole. Businesses are finding it more difficult to hire, and growth is being held back, while overcrowding and social problems are increasing. The Swedish housing market is characterised by long queues for rentals, high demands for buying a home and complicated rules for building. A better functioning housing market requires an efficient planning process, accessible land and flexible building rules. Then both the time from idea to migration and production costs can decrease and the supply of housing can thus increase. We Moderates want to contribute to a housing market where opportunities are at the centre. We protect property rights and want to strengthen the individual in relation to the public, so that the individual gets more advice over his or her property. Madam President, I would like There is a need for a broad review of the overall national framework for housing construction in order to speed up decision-making processes, increase flexibility and reduce production costs. Sweden has the highest construction costs in Europe. This is explained, among other things, by high wage costs and high demands on what is being built. At the same time, construction costs represent just over half of the total cost of production. In addition to the construction costs, there are costs for VAT, land acquisitions, municipal fees and also the costs of the developer or the client. Generally speaking, the profit margins in the Swedish construction industry are not higher than in the rest of Europe or in the business sector as a whole. At the same time, there are many studies that point to low productivity in the industry and that increased international and domestic competition could increase the efficiency of the industry and thus enable increased production. In the Swedish Competition Authority's report "Better competition in residential building" from 2018, the Authority identifies three areas that stand out to adversely affect competition and thus the efficiency of the industry: the building materials industry, the public procurement of residential building and the municipal special requirements. The material costs represent about half of the total construction costs. The costs of materials have increased rapidly over a number of years, and the Swedish Competition Authority notes that this is the main reason why the total construction costs have also risen. The rapid increase in costs in the construction materials industry indicates that there may be competition concerns. In April 2020, the Government commissioned the Swedish Competition Authority to investigate precisely the competition in the construction materials industry, including in terms of market concentration and price formation, and in December last year the result came. Competition in certain parts of the construction materials industry is inadequate. The problems are multiple and exist at different levels. These are high market concentration in key material markets, procurement in a manner that restricts competition and cost-driving discounts and remuneration conditions. These are some of the shortcomings identified by the Competition Authority in the report and which need to be addressed. Madam President, I would like The municipalities of our country also have an important role to play in increasing housing construction, while at the same time safeguarding municipal self-government, without government pointers and from above that risk undermining the acceptance and tolerance of the inhabitants of the municipality for the construction that is so badly needed. The State, for its part, should not hinder the construction of housing, but should facilitate as much as possible. The state can motivate municipalities to build, for example by linking infrastructure investments to residential building, which we Moderates are very positive about. Madam President, I would like We Moderates have no reservations in the report, as the proposals that we have put on Parliament's table are dealt with so-called simplified. The committee motion that we put on Parliament's table – Reforms for increased construction and a better functioning housing market – with 70 sharp proposals, however, shows what policy we want to pursue in a moderate-led government after the elections this autumn. In order for us to get Sweden in order, which is long-awaited by many, we also need to get order in the Swedish housing market and Swedish housing policy.

This has been made possible by the large-scale expansion of hydropower and nuclear power, which has reduced our fossil share in energy supply from almost 85% in the early 70s to just over a quarter today. This compares with the fact that the share of fossils in global energy supply remains at levels around 80 percent. There has not much happened in the corresponding period, almost half a century. Sweden is now facing an extensive electrification as the remaining fossil energy share is to be phased out. It is an expression that has become something of a mantra in energy policy when these issues are discussed. A very large majority in this House is in no way opposed to this. On the contrary, electricity is viewed positively as an enabler and energy carrier in order to phase out the fossil share and significantly increase our electricity use. It is excellent, Madam President, that I support our reservation 26. We are, of course, in favour of all our reservations, but, as is customary, I am only in favour of one. In October 2021, the investigation submitted a report entitled A legally secure wind test, SOU 2021:53. The report describes, among other things, the existence of so-called wind farm plans, i.e. a kind of thematic addition to the municipalities' outline plans. The opinion of the study is that the previous support for wind power planning has been of great importance in increasing knowledge and getting municipalities to plan for wind power. The investigation also notes that the identification in the master plan was of great importance for the examination of the permit prior to the introduction of the municipal veto right. However, the requirement for municipal power has reduced the value of identifying areas for wind power expansion in the master plan in both theory and practice. In theory, this can be done by municipalities being able to decide at any time during a review process not to support a wind power establishment regardless of what is stated in the master plan. In practice, it has also been shown that municipalities have not approved projects although these have been planned in areas that have been identified as suitable in outline plans. The investigation also presents the pros and cons of a model that makes municipal wind farm plans legally binding. It is a model that draws inspiration from the trial according to the Planning and Construction Act but which nevertheless constitutes a special regulation of wind power. The binding plans would remind us of today's wind farm plans, which are thus thematic additions to the outline plans. However, unlike these, they must be binding on both the municipality and others. In conclusion, the study's proposal means that municipalities continue to have the right to say no to wind power. However, unlike today, the municipality will provide information early in the process, and the scope of the municipality's decision is limited to land and water use only. The licensing authority then examines whether the wind farm can be granted a permit taking into account, for example, natural environmental interests. The report of the inquiry was sent out for consultation in the autumn of last year. The Left Party's view of the municipal veto has been known since before, and the government intends to come up with a bill early in April 2022. However, it does not come to our committee, which is why I mention it here. We realise, of course, that there is no support for abolishing the municipal veto. But compensation for municipalities when wind power is established must be provided, otherwise we miss the rate of renewable expansion. There should be something about this in the context of the wind energy review. Madam President, I would like There is so much that will depend on how we rig up our energy system for the next decade. Let me highlight a few things that need to be developed in order for us to have good care for people affected by dementia and other cognitive diseases. An expanded primary care where you are entitled to a fixed-named doctor's contact is of great importance in this case as well. You should know the name of your doctor, and he should have a reasonable number of patients, so that you can make an appointment when needed. If you get the possibility of continuity increases the chance that you as a patient dare to mention what feels strange but that you do not understand what it is. If there is continuity also increases the chance for the doctor to start an investigation in time to find out what it is that has emerged. If, after an investigation, you are told that you have a dementia of some kind, it is important to immediately get a follow-up and feel cared for. It is a revolutionary message to know that you have, for example, Alzheimer's disease. It's a chronic and fatal disease. What treatment can be given, and what effect does it have? What can I do to make myself feel better? At that stage, meeting a counselor and perhaps an occupational therapist could be worth gold. Dementia nurses are in some places, and this is a highly appreciated function. You get help to deal with the first time after the announcement and make yourself and perhaps also close relatives more prepared for the disease that will gradually develop. These are examples of what may need to be included in national guidelines for dementia. It is important that they are developed in broad collaboration between patient organisations, professions, researchers and authorities.
